Evolution of the phase diagram in the
t^{1}_{x}-\alpha^{i}
parameter space as a function of the angle of the magnetic field
\gamma
, when the Weyl cones are tilted opposite to each other.

Figure A.4:
Normalized planar Hall conductivity (
\sigma_{xz}^{\prime}
) as a function of the angle
\gamma
for several values of tilt parameter
t_{z}
for oppositely tilted Weyl cones. In the absence of tilt the behavior follows the trend
\sin(2\gamma)
, while in the presence of tilt, a
\cos\gamma
component is added. Beyond a critical
t_{z}^{c}
, the
\cos\gamma
term dominates and
\sigma_{xz}^{\prime}(\pi/2+\epsilon)
changes from positive to negative, where
\epsilon
is a small positive angle.
A finite intervalley scattering further enhances the
\cos\gamma
trend (however only in the presence of a finite tilt). It’s effect is to lower the critical tilt
t_{z}^{c}
where the sign change occurs.

Figure A.5:
Normalized planar Hall conductivity (
\sigma_{xz}^{\prime}
) as a function of the angle
\gamma
for several values of tilt parameter
t_{x}
for oppositely tilted Weyl cones. In the absence of tilt the behavior follows the trend
\sin(2\gamma)
, while in the presence of tilt, a
\sin\gamma
component is added. Beyond a critical
t_{x}^{c}
, the
\sin\gamma
term dominates and
\sigma_{xz}^{\prime}(\pi/2+\epsilon)
changes from positive to negative, where
\epsilon
is a small positive angle.
A finite intervalley scattering further enhances the
\sin\gamma
trend (however only in the presence of a finite tilt). It’s effect is to lower the critical tilt
t_{x}^{c}
where the sign change occurs.

A.2
Boltzmann transport equation

The Boltzmann equation is reduced to the following form

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{A}\mathbb{Z}-\mathbb{Y},

(A.8)

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda^{+}\\
a^{+}\\
b^{+}\\
c^{+}\\
\lambda^{-}\\
a^{-}\\
b^{-}\\
c^{-}\\
\end{pmatrix}

(A.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{++}F^{+}&\alpha^{++}G^{+}&\alpha^{++}I^{+}&\alpha^{++}J^{+}&\alpha^{+-}F^{-}&\alpha^{+-}G^{-}&\alpha^{+-}I^{-}&\alpha^{+-}J^{-}\\
\alpha^{++}G^{+}&\alpha^{++}O^{+}&\alpha^{++}P^{+}&\alpha^{++}Q^{+}&\alpha^{+-}G^{-}&\alpha^{+-}O^{-}&\alpha^{+-}P^{-}&\alpha^{+-}Q^{-}\\
\alpha^{++}I^{+}&\alpha^{++}P^{+}&\alpha^{++}S^{+}&\alpha^{++}U^{+}&\alpha^{+-}I^{-}&\alpha^{+-}P^{-}&\alpha^{+-}S^{-}&\alpha^{+-}U^{-}\\
\alpha^{++}J^{+}&\alpha^{++}Q^{+}&\alpha^{++}U^{+}&\alpha^{++}V^{+}&\alpha^{+-}J^{-}&\alpha^{+-}Q^{-}&\alpha^{+-}U^{-}&\alpha^{+-}V^{-}\\
\alpha^{-+}F^{+}&\alpha^{-+}G^{+}&\alpha^{-+}I^{+}&\alpha^{-+}J^{+}&\alpha^{--}F^{-}&\alpha^{--}G^{-}&\alpha^{--}I^{-}&\alpha^{--}J^{-}\\
\alpha^{-+}G^{+}&\alpha^{-+}O^{+}&\alpha^{-+}P^{+}&\alpha^{-+}Q^{+}&\alpha^{--}G^{-}&\alpha^{--}O^{-}&\alpha^{--}P^{-}&\alpha^{--}Q^{-}\\
\alpha^{-+}I^{+}&\alpha^{-+}P^{+}&\alpha^{-+}S^{+}&\alpha^{-+}U^{+}&\alpha^{--}I^{-}&\alpha^{--}P^{-}&\alpha^{--}S^{-}&\alpha^{--}U^{-}\\
\alpha^{-+}J^{+}&\alpha^{-+}Q^{+}&\alpha^{-+}U^{+}&\alpha^{-+}V^{+}&\alpha^{--}J^{-}&\alpha^{--}Q^{-}&\alpha^{--}U^{-}&\alpha^{--}V^{-}\\
\end{pmatrix}

(A.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Y}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{++}H^{+}+\alpha^{+-}H^{-}\\
\alpha^{++}N^{+}-\alpha^{+-}N^{-}\\
\alpha^{++}L^{+}-\alpha^{+-}L^{-}\\
\alpha^{-+}M^{+}-\alpha^{--}M^{-}\\
\alpha^{--}H^{+}+\alpha^{-+}H^{-}\\
\alpha^{--}N^{-}-\alpha^{-+}N^{+}\\
\alpha^{--}L^{-}-\alpha^{-+}L^{+}\\
\alpha^{--}M^{-}-\alpha^{-+}M^{+}\\
\end{pmatrix}

(A.11)

The relevant integrals involved in the above matrices are:

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=F^{\chi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})h^{\chi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=H^{\chi^{\prime}}

(A.12)

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\cos{\theta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=G^{\chi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\sin{\theta^{\prime}}\cos{\phi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=I^{\chi^{\prime}}

(A.13)

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\sin{\theta^{\prime}}\sin{\phi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=J^{\chi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\sin^{2}{\theta^{\prime}}\cos^{2}{\phi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=S^{\chi^{\prime}}

(A.14)

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})h^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\cos{\theta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=N^{\chi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})h^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\sin{\theta^{\prime}}\cos{\phi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=L^{\chi^{\prime}}

(A.15)

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})h^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\sin{\theta^{\prime}}\sin{\phi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=M^{\chi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\cos^{2}{\theta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=O^{\chi^{\prime}}

(A.16)

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\sin{\theta^{\prime}}\cos{\theta^{\prime}}\cos{\phi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=P^{\chi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\sin{\theta^{\prime}}\cos{\theta^{\prime}}\sin{\phi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=Q^{\chi^{\prime}}

(A.17)

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\sin^{2}{\theta^{\prime}}\cos{\phi^{\prime}}\sin{\phi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=U^{\chi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\iint d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}f^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\sin^{2}{\theta^{\prime}}\sin^{2}{\phi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=V^{\chi^{\prime}}

(A.18)

A.3
Evolution of the LMC phase diagram with magnetic field

In Fig.
A.1
we plot the evolution of the contour shape in the
t^{1}_{z}-\alpha^{i}
parameter space as a function of the angle of the magnetic field
\gamma
. When
\gamma
is directed away from
\pi/2
the shape of the zero LMC contour looks like a curved trapezoid instead of
U
. The critical value
\alpha_{i}^{c}
where the sign change first occurs is seen to reduce and elongate its region from
|t_{z}|\approx 0.5
when
\gamma=\pi/2
to
|t_{z}|\approx 1
as
\gamma\rightarrow 0
. In FIg.
A.2
we plot the evolution of the contour shape in the
t^{1}_{x}-\alpha^{i}
parameter space as a function of the angle of the magnetic field
\gamma
. It is noted that region of negative LMC expands in the parameter space along with the reduction of the critical intervalley strength
\alpha_{i}^{c}
where the sign change first
occurs. The reduction of the critical intervalley strength can again be understood as a combination of the two factors (i) a finite tilt and intervalley scattering
(when
\gamma=\pi/2
) drives the system to change the LMC sign from positive to negative, and
secondly directing the magnetic field away from the
z
-axis in the presence of intervalley scattering (in the absence of tilt) drives the system to change LMC sign from positive to
negative much below the critical intervalley strength. The different shape of the contour (negative LMC filling out the parameter space instead of a curved trapezoid) is essentially because the cones are now tilted along the
x
-direction and
the magnetic field has an
x
-component to it, which is
qualitatively different from the tilt occurring in the
z
-direction. Finally in Fig.
A.3
we plot the evolution of the phase diagram when the Weyl cones are tilted along the
x
direction but oriented opposite to each other. Directing the magnetic field even slightly away from the
z
-axis changes the qualitative behavior since a linear-in-
B
component is added in the LMC response. This is because the magnetic field now has a finite component along the tilt direction, and the tilts are oppositely oriented to each
other.

A.4
Angular dependence on PHC

In Fig.
A.4
we plot the normalized planar Hall conductivity (
\sigma_{xz}^{\prime}
) as a function of the angle
\gamma
for several values of tilt parameter
t_{z}
for oppositely tilted Weyl cones. In the absence of tilt the behavior follows the trend
\sin(2\gamma)
, while in the presence of tilt, a
\cos\gamma
component is added. Beyond a critical
t_{z}^{c}
, the
\cos\gamma
term dominates and
\sigma_{xz}^{\prime}(\pi/2+\epsilon)
changes from positive to negative, where
\epsilon
is a small positive angle. A finite intervalley scattering further enhances the
\cos\gamma
trend (however only in the presence of a finite tilt). Its effect is to lower the critical tilt
t_{z}^{c}
where the sign change occurs.

In Fig.
A.5
we plot the normalized planar Hall conductivity as a function of the angle
\gamma
for several values of tilt parameter
t_{x}
for oppositely tilted Weyl cones. In the absence of tilt the behavior follows the expected trend of
\sin(2\gamma)
, while in the presence of tilt, a
\sin\gamma
component is added. Beyond a critical value of the tilt (
t_{x}^{c}
), the
\sin\gamma
term dominates the behavior
\sigma_{xz}^{\prime}
never changes sign as a function of the parameter
\gamma
. A finite intervalley scattering further enhances the
\sin\gamma
trend (however only in the presence of a finite tilt). Its effect is to lower the critical value of the tilt
t_{x}^{c}
.

A.5
Boltzmann transport for a system with multiple nodes

For a system with multiple Weyl nodes, the distribution function at each node can be represented by
f_{\mathbf{k}}^{m}
. Generalizing the formalism presented in the main text, the collision integral must take into account scattering between multiple Weyl cones that may or may not be of the same chirality or tilt. Thus
\mathcal{I}_{{col}}[f^{m}_{\mathbf{k}}]
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{col}}[f^{m}_{\mathbf{k}}]=\sum\limits_{p}\sum\limits_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}W^{mp}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(f^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-f^{m}_{\mathbf{k}}),

(A.19)

where the sum
p
runs over nodes, and scattering rate
W^{mp}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
in the first Born approximation is given by

\displaystyle W^{mp}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}\frac{n}{\mathcal{V}}|\langle\psi^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|U^{mp}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|\psi^{m}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle|^{2}\delta(\epsilon^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{F})

(A.20)

The scattering potential profile
U^{mp}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
can be chosen such that scattering between all or some of the nodes (internode) as well as within each node (intranode) is considered. Proceeding as before, we define the valley scattering time
{\tau^{m}_{\mathbf{k}}}
as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)}=\mathcal{V}\sum\limits_{p}\iint{\frac{\beta^{mp}(k^{\prime})^{3}}{|\mathbf{v}^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{k}^{\prime p}|}\sin\theta^{\prime}\mathcal{G}^{mp}(\mathcal{D}^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})^{-1}d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}},

(A.21)

and the Boltzmann equation becomes

\displaystyle h^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)+\frac{\Lambda^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)}{\tau^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}\sum_{p}\iint{\frac{\beta^{mp}(k^{\prime})^{3}}{|\mathbf{v}^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{k}^{\prime p}|}\sin\theta^{\prime}\mathcal{G}^{mp}(\mathcal{D}^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})^{-1}\Lambda^{p}_{\mu}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}}.

(A.22)

Making the ansatz
\Lambda^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)=(\lambda^{m}-h^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)+a^{m}\cos\theta+b^{m}\sin\theta\cos\phi+c^{m}\sin\theta\sin\phi)\tau^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)
, and using the particle number conservation constraint, the Boltzmann equation is reduced to a system of
4N
equations to be solved for
4N
unknowns (
N
being the number of nodes).

Appendix A

A.1
Boltzmann formalism for magnetotransport

Using the quasiclassical Boltzmann theory, we study transport in Weyl semimetals in the limit of weak electric and magnetic fields. Since quasiclassical Boltzmann theory is valid away from the nodal point such that
\mu^{2}\gg\hbar v_{F}^{2}eB
, therefore without any loss of generality we will assume that the chemical potential lies in the conduction band. The phenomenological Boltzmann equation for the non-equilibrium distribution function
f^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}
can be expressed as
[
97
]

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\dot{\mathbf{r}}^{\chi}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{r}}+\dot{\mathbf{k}}^{\chi}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{k}}\right)f^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{coll}}[f^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}],

(A.1)

where the collision term on the right-hand side of the equation incorporates the effects of scattering due to impurities.
In the presence of electric (
\mathbf{E}
) and magnetic (
\mathbf{B}
) fields, the semiclassical dynamics of the Bloch electrons is
[
96
]

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{r}}^{\chi}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}^{\chi}\left(\frac{e}{\hbar}(\mathbf{E}\times\bm{\Omega}^{\chi}+\frac{e}{\hbar}(\mathbf{v}^{\chi}\cdot\bm{\Omega}^{\chi})\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\chi})\right)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{p}}^{\chi}

\displaystyle=-e\mathcal{D}^{\chi}\left(\mathbf{E}+\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\chi}\times\mathbf{B}+\frac{e}{\hbar}(\mathbf{E}\cdot\mathbf{B})\bm{\Omega}^{\chi}\right),

(A.2)

where
\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\chi}
is the band velocity,
\bm{\Omega}^{\chi}=-\chi\mathbf{k}/2k^{3}
is the Berry curvature, and
\mathcal{D}^{\chi}=(1+e\mathbf{B}\cdot\bm{\Omega}^{\chi}/\hbar)^{-1}
. The self-rotation of Bloch wavepacket also gives rise to an orbital magnetic moment (OMM)
[
82
]

\mathbf{m}^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}
. In the presence of magnetic field, the OMM shifts the energy dispersion as
\epsilon^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}\rightarrow\epsilon^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}-\mathbf{m}^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}\cdot\mathbf{B}
. Interestingly, the Berry curvature and the orbital magnetic moment turn out to be independent of the tilting of the Weyl cones.

The collision integral must take into account scattering between the two Weyl nodes (internode,
\chi\Longleftrightarrow\chi^{\prime}
), as well as scattering withing a Weyl node (intranode,
\chi\Longleftrightarrow\chi
), and thus
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{coll}}[f^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}]
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{coll}}[f^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}]=\sum\limits_{\chi^{\prime}}\sum\limits_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}W^{\chi\chi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(f^{\chi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-f^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}),

(A.3)

where the scattering rate
W^{\chi\chi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
is given by
[
97
]

\displaystyle W^{\chi\chi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}\frac{n}{\mathcal{V}}|\langle\psi^{\chi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|U^{\chi\chi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|\psi^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle|^{2}\delta(\epsilon^{\chi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{F})

(A.4)

In the above expression
n
is the impurity concentration,
\mathcal{V}
is the system volume,
|\psi^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle
is the Weyl spinor wavefunction (which is obtained by diagonalizing the low-energy Weyl Hamiltonian given in the main text),
U^{\chi\chi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
is the scattering potential, and
\epsilon_{F}
is the Fermi energy. The scattering potential profile
U^{\chi\chi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
is determined by the nature of impurities. Here we restrict ourselves to only non-magnetic point-like impurity, but distinguish between intervalley and intravalley scattering. This can be controlled independently in our formalism. Thus, the scattering matrix is momentum-independent but has a dependence on the chirality, i.e.,
U^{\chi\chi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=U^{\chi\chi^{\prime}}\mathbb{I}
.

The distribution function is assumed to take the form
f^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}=f_{0}^{\chi}+g^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}
, where
f_{0}^{\chi}
is the equilibrium Fermi-Dirac distribution function and
g^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}
indicates the deviation from equilibrium.
In the steady state, the Boltzmann equation (Eq.
A.1
) takes the following form

\displaystyle\left[\left(\frac{\partial f_{0}^{\chi}}{\partial\epsilon^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}}\right)\mathbf{E}\cdot\left(\mathbf{v}^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}+\frac{e\mathbf{B}}{\hbar}(\bm{\Omega}^{\chi}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{e\mathcal{D}^{\chi}}\sum\limits_{\chi^{\prime}}\sum\limits_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}W^{\chi\chi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(g^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-g^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}})

(A.5)

The deviation
g^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}
is assumed to be linearly proportional to the applied electric field

\displaystyle g^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}=e\left(-\frac{\partial f_{0}^{\chi}}{\partial\epsilon^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}}\right)\mathbf{E}\cdot\bm{\Lambda}^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}

(A.6)

We fix the direction of the applied external electric field to be along
+\hat{z}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{E}=E\hat{z}
. Therefore only
{\Lambda}^{\chi z}_{\mathbf{k}}\equiv{\Lambda}^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}
, is relevant. Further, we rotate the magnetic field along the
xz
-plane such that it makes an angle
\gamma
with respect to the
\hat{x}-
axis, i.e.,
\mathbf{B}=B(\cos\gamma,0,\sin\gamma)
. When
\gamma=\pi/2
, the electric and magnetic fields are parallel to each other. Similarly, the strain induced chiral gauge field is rotated in the
xz
-plane, i.e,.
\mathbf{B_{5}}^{\chi}=\chi B_{5}(\cos\gamma_{5},0,\sin\gamma_{5})
.
When
\gamma_{5}\neq\pi/2
, the electric and gauge field are non-collinear and this geometry will be useful in analyzing the strain induced planar Hall effect. Thus the net magnetic field at each valley becomes
\mathbf{B}^{\chi}\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}+\chi\mathbf{B_{5}}
.

Keeping terms only up to linear order in the electric field, Eq.
A.5
takes the following form

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\chi}\left[v^{\chi z}_{\mathbf{k}}+\frac{eB}{\hbar}\sin\gamma(\bm{\Omega}^{\chi}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}})\right]=\sum\limits_{\eta}\sum\limits_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}W^{\eta\chi}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(\Lambda^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-\Lambda^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}})

(A.7)

In order to solve the above equation, we first define the valley scattering rate as follows

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}}=\mathcal{V}\sum\limits_{\eta}\int{\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{3}}(\mathcal{D}^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})^{-1}W^{\eta\chi}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}

(A.8)

Due to the tilting of the Weyl cones the azimuthal symmetry is destroyed even when the electric and magnetic fields are parallel to each other, and therefore all the integrations are performed over both
\theta
and
\phi
. The radial integration is simplified due to the delta-function in Eq.
A.4
.

Substituting the scattering rate from Eq.
A.4
in the above equation, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}}=\frac{\mathcal{V}N}{8\pi^{2}\hbar}\sum\limits_{\eta}|U^{\chi\eta}|^{2}\iiint{(k^{\prime})^{2}\sin\theta^{\prime}\mathcal{G}^{\chi\eta}(\theta,\phi,\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\delta(\epsilon^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{F})(\mathcal{D}^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})^{-1}dk^{\prime}d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}},

(A.9)

where
N
now indicates the total number of impurities, and
\mathcal{G}^{\chi\eta}(\theta,\phi,\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})=(1+\chi\eta(\cos\theta\cos\theta^{\prime}+\sin\theta\sin\theta^{\prime}\cos(\phi-\phi^{\prime})))
is the Weyl chirality factor defined by the overlap of the wavefunctions. The Fermi wavevector contour
k^{\chi}
is evaluated by equating the energy expression with the Fermi energy.
The three-dimensional integral in Eq.
A.9
is reduced to just integration in
\phi^{\prime}
and
\theta^{\prime}
. The scattering time
{\tau^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}}
depends on the chemical potential (
\mu
), and is a function of the angular variables
\theta
and
\phi
.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau^{\chi}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)}=\mathcal{V}\sum\limits_{\eta}\iint{\frac{\beta^{\chi\eta}(k^{\prime})^{3}}{|\mathbf{v}^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{k}^{\prime\eta}|}\sin\theta^{\prime}\mathcal{G}^{\chi\eta}(\mathcal{D}^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})^{-1}d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}},

(A.10)

where
\beta^{\chi\eta}=N|U^{\chi\eta}|^{2}/4\pi^{2}\hbar^{2}
. The Boltzmann equation (Eq
A.7
) assumes the form

\displaystyle h^{\chi}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)+\frac{\Lambda^{\chi}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)}{\tau^{\chi}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}\sum_{\eta}\iint{\frac{\beta^{\chi\eta}(k^{\prime})^{3}}{|\mathbf{v}^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{k}^{\prime\eta}|}\sin\theta^{\prime}\mathcal{G}^{\chi\eta}(\mathcal{D}^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})^{-1}\Lambda^{\eta}_{\mu}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}}

(A.11)

We make the following ansatz for
\Lambda^{\chi}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\chi}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=(\lambda^{\chi}-h^{\chi}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)+a^{\chi}\cos\theta+

\displaystyle b^{\chi}\sin\theta\cos\phi+c^{\chi}\sin\theta\sin\phi)\tau^{\chi}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi),

(A.12)

where we solve for the eight unknowns (
\lambda^{\pm 1},a^{\pm 1},b^{\pm 1},c^{\pm 1}
). The L.H.S in Eq.
A.11
simplifies to
\lambda^{\chi}+a^{\chi}\cos\theta+b^{\chi}\sin\theta\cos\phi+c^{\chi}\sin\theta\sin\phi
. The R.H.S of Eq.
A.11
simplifies to

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}\sum_{\eta}\beta^{\chi\eta}\iint

\displaystyle f^{\eta}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\mathcal{G}^{\chi\eta}(\lambda^{\eta}-h^{\eta}_{\mu}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})+a^{\eta}\cos\theta^{\prime}+

\displaystyle b^{\eta}\sin\theta^{\prime}\cos\phi^{\prime}+c^{\eta}\sin\theta^{\prime}\sin\phi^{\prime})d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime},

(A.13)

where the function

\displaystyle f^{\eta}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})=\frac{(k^{\prime})^{3}}{|\mathbf{v}^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{k}^{\prime\eta}|}\sin\theta^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})^{-1}\tau^{\chi}_{\mu}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})

(A.14)

The above equations, when written down explicitly take the form of seven simultaneous equations to be solved for eight variables. The final constraint comes from the particle number conservation

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\chi}\sum\limits_{\mathbf{k}}g^{\chi}_{\mathbf{k}}=0

(A.15)

Eq.
A.12
, Eq.
A.13
, Eq.
A.14
and Eq.
A.15
are solved together with Eq
A.10
, simultaneously for the eight unknowns (
\lambda^{\pm 1},a^{\pm 1},b^{\pm 1},c^{\pm 1}
). Due to the complicated nature of the equations, all the two dimensional integrals w.r.t {
\theta^{\prime}
,
\phi^{\prime}
}, and the solution of the simultaneous equations are performed numerically.

For the inversion asymmetric WSM with four Weyl nodes, the distribution function at each node can be represented by
f_{\mathbf{k}}^{m}
. Generalizing the formalism presented above, the collision integral must take into account scattering between multiple Weyl cones. Thus
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{coll}}[f^{m}_{\mathbf{k}}]
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{coll}}[f^{m}_{\mathbf{k}}]=\sum\limits_{p}\sum\limits_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}W^{mp}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(f^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-f^{m}_{\mathbf{k}}),

(A.16)

where
p
runs over all the nodes, and scattering rate
W^{mp}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
is given by

\displaystyle W^{mp}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}\frac{n}{\mathcal{V}}|\langle\psi^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|U^{mp}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|\psi^{m}_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle|^{2}\delta(\epsilon^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{F})

(A.17)

The scattering potential profile
U^{mp}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
can be chosen such that scattering between the nodes (internode) as well as within each node (intranode) is considered. Proceeding as before, we define
{\tau^{m}_{\mathbf{k}}}
as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)}=\mathcal{V}\sum\limits_{p}\iint{\frac{\beta^{mp}(k^{\prime})^{3}}{|\mathbf{v}^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{k}^{\prime p}|}\sin\theta^{\prime}\mathcal{G}^{mp}(\mathcal{D}^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})^{-1}d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}},

(A.18)

and the Boltzmann equation becomes

\displaystyle h^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)+\frac{\Lambda^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)}{\tau^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}\sum_{p}\iint{\frac{\beta^{mp}(k^{\prime})^{3}}{|\mathbf{v}^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{k}^{\prime p}|}\sin\theta^{\prime}\mathcal{G}^{mp}(\mathcal{D}^{p}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})^{-1}\Lambda^{p}_{\mu}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})d\theta^{\prime}d\phi^{\prime}}.

(A.19)

Making the ansatz
\Lambda^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)=(\lambda^{m}-h^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)+a^{m}\cos\theta+b^{m}\sin\theta\cos\phi+c^{m}\sin\theta\sin\phi)\tau^{m}_{\mu}(\theta,\phi)
, and using the constraint for particle number conservation, the Boltzmann equation is reduced to a system of sixteen equations to be solved for sixteen unknowns.

Appendix A

A.1
Substitution and expression for system of linear equations

The system of linear equations of the unknown variables involved in the
\Lambda^{\chi}
is written as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}a^{+}\\
b^{+}\\
c^{+}\\
a^{-}\\
b^{-}\\
c^{-}\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\Pi^{++}T^{+}&\Pi^{++}I^{+}&\Pi^{++}J^{+}&\Pi^{+-}I^{-}&\Pi^{+-}M^{-}&\Pi^{+-}N^{-}\\
\Pi^{++}I^{+}&\Pi^{++}M^{+}&\Pi^{++}N^{+}&\Pi^{+-}T^{-}&\Pi^{+-}I^{-}&\Pi^{+-}J^{-}\\
\Pi^{++}J^{+}&\Pi^{++}N^{+}&\Pi^{++}Q^{+}&\Pi^{+-}J^{-}&\Pi^{+-}N^{-}&\Pi^{+-}Q^{-}\\
\Pi^{-+}I^{+}&\Pi^{-+}M^{+}&\Pi^{-+}N^{+}&\Pi^{--}T^{-}&\Pi^{--}I^{-}&\Pi^{--}J^{-}\\
\Pi^{-+}T^{+}&\Pi^{-+}I^{+}&\Pi^{-+}J^{+}&\Pi^{--}I^{-}&\Pi^{--}M^{-}&\Pi^{--}N^{-}\\
\Pi^{-+}J^{+}&\Pi^{-+}N^{+}&\Pi^{-+}Q^{+}&\Pi^{--}J^{-}&\Pi^{--}N^{-}&\Pi^{--}Q^{-}\\
\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}a^{+}\\
b^{+}\\
c^{+}\\
a^{-}\\
b^{-}\\
c^{-}\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle-\begin{bmatrix}\Pi^{++}G^{+}+\Pi^{+-}L^{-}\\
\Pi^{++}L^{+}+\Pi^{+-}G^{-}\\
\Pi^{++}P^{+}/4+\Pi^{+-}P^{-}/4\\
\Pi^{-+}L^{+}+\Pi^{--}G^{-}\\
\Pi^{-+}G^{+}+\Pi^{--}L^{-}\\
\Pi^{-+}P^{+}/4+\Pi^{--}P^{-}/4\\
\end{bmatrix}

(A.1)

Substitution in the above matrix equation is written as:

\displaystyle\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})\cos^{4}(\theta^{\prime}/2)

\displaystyle=R^{\chi},\quad\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})\sin^{4}(\theta^{\prime}/2)=S^{\chi},

\displaystyle\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})\sin^{8}(\theta^{\prime}/2)

\displaystyle=M^{\chi},\quad\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})h^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})=U^{\chi},

\displaystyle\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})\cos^{8}(\theta^{\prime}/2)

\displaystyle=T^{\chi},\quad\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})\sin^{2}(\theta^{\prime})=O^{\chi},

\displaystyle\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})h^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})\sin^{2}(\theta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=P^{\chi},\quad\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})h^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})\sin^{4}(\theta^{\prime}/2)=L^{\chi},

\displaystyle\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})h^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})\cos^{4}(\theta^{\prime}/2)

\displaystyle=G^{\chi},

\displaystyle\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})h^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})\cos^{4}(\theta^{\prime}/2)\sin^{2}(\theta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=J^{\chi},

\displaystyle\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})h^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})\sin^{4}(\theta^{\prime}/2)\sin^{2}(\theta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=N^{\chi},

\displaystyle\quad\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})h^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})\sin^{4}(\theta^{\prime}/2)\cos^{4}(\theta^{\prime}/2)

\displaystyle=I^{\chi},

\displaystyle\int d\theta^{\prime}f^{\chi}(\theta^{\prime})\sin^{4}(\theta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=Q^{\chi}.

A.2
Zero-field conductivity

We calculate the zero-field conductivity and derive its analytical expressions for two systems, i.e., spin-1/2 and pseudospin-1 using the formalism mentioned in the main text. For
\mathbf{B}=0
, the the coupled Eq.
4.5
acquire the following form:

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{r}}^{\chi}

\displaystyle=\frac{e}{\hbar}(\mathbf{E}\times\bm{\Omega}^{\chi})+\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\chi}

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{k}}^{\chi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{e}{\hbar}\penalty\ \mathbf{E}.

(A.2)

Using this, the Boltzmann equation is simplified to:

\displaystyle v^{\chi}_{z,\mathbf{k}}+\frac{\Lambda^{\chi}(\theta)}{\tau^{\chi}_{\mu}}=\sum_{\chi^{\prime}}\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\mathbf{W}^{\chi\chi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{kk^{\prime}}}\Lambda^{\chi^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime}).

(A.3)

We solve this analytically using the ansatz defined in the main text and using the ansatz defined in Ref.
[
66
,
53
]
for Weyl (spin-1/2). The net scattering rate at two valleys (
\chi=\pm 1
) for
B=0
is calculated to be:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau^{\chi,s=1}_{\mu}}=\frac{4\pi V\epsilon_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}(\alpha+1)}{3\hbar^{3}v_{\mathrm{F}}^{3}}\equiv\frac{8}{\tau^{\chi,s=1/2}_{\mu}}.

(A.4)

Returning back to Eq.
A.3
, it is written explicitly in terms of unknown variables involved in ansatz for spin-1 WSMs as:

\lambda^{+}-\frac{\sin^{2}(\theta)\left(10\lambda^{+}+3a^{+}+3b^{+}-12c^{+}+10\lambda^{-}\alpha+3\alpha a^{-}+3\alpha b^{-}-20\alpha c^{+}+8\alpha c^{-}\right)}{20(\alpha+1)}\\
-\frac{\cos^{4}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\left(10\lambda^{+}-4a^{+}+b^{+}+6c^{+}-5v_{\mathrm{F}}+10\lambda^{-}\alpha-10\alpha a^{+}+\alpha a^{-}+6\alpha b^{-}+6\alpha c^{-}+5\alpha v_{\mathrm{F}}\right)}{10(\alpha+1)}\\
-\frac{\sin^{4}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\left(10\lambda^{+}+a^{+}-4b^{+}+6c^{+}+5v_{\mathrm{F}}+10\lambda^{-}\alpha+6\alpha a^{-}-10\alpha b^{+}+\alpha b^{-}+6\alpha c^{-}-5\alpha v_{\mathrm{F}}\right)}{10(\alpha+1)}=0

(A.5)

\lambda^{-}-\frac{\sin^{2}(\theta)\big(10\lambda^{-}+3a^{-}+3b^{-}-12c^{-}+10\lambda^{+}\alpha+3\alpha a^{+}+3\alpha b^{+}+8\alpha c^{+}-20\alpha c^{-}\big)}{20(\alpha+1)}\\
-\frac{\cos^{4}\big(\frac{\theta}{2}\big)\big(10\lambda^{-}-4a^{-}+b^{-}+6c^{-}-5v_{\mathrm{F}}+10\lambda^{+}\alpha+\alpha a^{+}-10\alpha a^{-}+6\alpha b^{+}+6\alpha c^{+}+5\alpha v_{\mathrm{F}}\big)}{10(\alpha+1)}\\
-\frac{\sin^{4}\big(\frac{\theta}{2}\big)\big(10\lambda^{-}+a^{-}-4b^{-}+6c^{-}+5v_{\mathrm{F}}+10\lambda^{+}\alpha+6\alpha a^{+}+\alpha b^{+}-10\alpha b^{-}+6\alpha c^{+}-5\alpha v_{\mathrm{F}}\big)}{10(\alpha+1)}=0.\\

(A.6)

Equating the coefficient of
\cos^{4}\big(\frac{\theta}{2}\big),\penalty\ \sin^{4}\big(\frac{\theta}{2}\big)
and
\sin^{2}(\theta)
in these two equations results in a system of eight coupled equations to be solved for eight unknown variables (
\lambda^{\pm},a^{\pm},b^{\pm},c^{\pm}
). The solution obtained is as follows:
\lambda^{\pm}=0,a^{\pm}=\frac{v_{\mathrm{F}}(\alpha-1)}{3\alpha+1},b^{\pm}=a^{\pm},c^{\pm}=0
. Using Eq.
5.10
, Eq.
4.8
and Eq.
A.4
LMC is evaluated to be:

\displaystyle\sigma_{zz}^{s=1}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{2}v_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{V\pi^{2}\left(3\alpha+1\right)},

\displaystyle\sigma_{zz}^{s=1/2}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{2}v_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{16V\pi^{2}\left(2\alpha+1\right)}.

(A.7)
